With `tests/it.rs` mounting `src/sys/mod.rs`, which declares `#[cfg_attr(unix, path = "unix.rs")] mod imp;`, `crate::helper()` in `src/sys/unix.rs` got an `Exact` `CALLS` edge to the library's `helper` although rustc compiles that file into the test crate as well; it is now left unresolved, and the `relationship_resolution` quality note counts the file. A `#[path]` the index cannot read inside such a subtree now marks files of its package for the mounting crate, and is counted once in that note however many crates mount the subtree. The default location of a module whose only `path` sits in a `cfg_attr` (used when the condition is false) is still not followed. On this repository's own index and on a large async-runtime crate (555 Rust files) no authoritative edge changes. `RELATIONSHIP_RESOLVER_SEMANTICS_VERSION` is now `ri3-relationship-resolver-v19`, so an existing index reports `RebuildRequired` and must be rebuilt with `ok index`. One Rust `CALLS` conformance case covers the layout (compact count 125).
- `ok snapshot import` no longer leaves traces of a path the local policy excludes. Symbol-registry resolutions and similarity links other files held to the removed file's symbols named them by qualified name (`internal::vault::keys::KeyAnchored`), which no path glob matches, so they survived with their graph nodes and edges; they are now withdrawn by the names the removed symbols carried, unless an indexed file still defines the name, and counted (not named) in a `symbol_registry_caveat` quality note and an import caveat, since those uses now read as unresolved. An admitted file's own import of such a name is kept, as `ok index` keeps it. Graph nodes no file owns that the removal leaves with no edge, and call-site dictionary entries only removed call sites used (their ids spell the removed path), are removed. The artifact's quality notes about removed content (an import-resolver caveat in a removed file, an unresolved name in a removed chunk, a symbol-registry caveat for a name no remaining chunk uses) are dropped from the imported manifest. History hotspots are recomputed from the touches that remain, so a directory hotspot for a directory only removed files were in is gone, and parent directories' counts no longer include the removed touches. The staged database is compacted with `VACUUM` after the artifact's manifest is withheld when the policy step removed anything or the artifact carries free pages (a `--quality fast` artifact copies the exporter's), so removed names no longer stay readable in free pages; this costs one rewrite of the staged database and free disk space of about twice its size while it runs (#549).
- A change contract item with no evidence refs of its own (a required test with none, the `manual-validation` entry, a traceability entry whose plan section is empty) cites three of the plan's refs chosen by authority - exact-reference results first, then `symbol:` anchors, then `edge:` graph edges, then the rest - as the plan's own ref caps do since #433, listed in file order. It took the first three in string order, where `edge:` sorts ahead of `search:` and `symbol:`, so a graph edge could displace an exact reference (#537).
- `ok search` and MCP `search_code` keep each evidence ref beside the line it names in `code`, `semantic` and `hybrid` mode, through the pairing helper context packs use. Hybrid mode's merge of a path's semantic hit into its lexical one, and the git co-change annotation, appended lines and refs as two separately deduplicated lists: two hits on one chunk both number their first line `search:<path>:<range>:0`, so the semantic line was kept and its ref dropped, and two co-change facts stating the same line added one line and two refs. A merged line whose positional ref is taken is now renumbered under its chunk, and a merged line the result already states is not added again, with its ref: hybrid `evidence_refs` no longer lists the path's other semantic chunks, which named no line and contributed no score (a hybrid result could carry far more refs than lines, such as 7 lines and 59 refs; it now carries one ref per line), and co-change facts that state the same line leave one line citing the first of them, with every fact still on the `similar_change_overlap` component. Both surfaces share the one ranked search, so they stay identical; `graph` mode results are unchanged and still cite their graph-node document once. `docs/context-pack-spec.md` now documents that score component and retrieval trace ids are chunk-level ids of the index hit, not pack record ids, and how to map them; they are unchanged (#537).
- `ok index` no longer imports symbols or occurrences from a SCIP document whose path is secret-like or matches `[paths] deny`, or is not repository-relative (absolute, or with a `..` segment; `\` separators are read as `/`). They had no file row, but a SCIP symbol's qualified name is the full symbol string, which spells the module path, and its display name is the file's content, so MCP `search_symbols` and `ok symbol find` served both for files the policy excludes; the denied file's references also counted as exact references. The number of withheld documents is recorded as a `scip` quality note, without their paths. SCIP documents for files discovery skipped for other reasons (generated or `.gitignore`d code) are still imported, so an indexed file's reference into them resolves. `ok snapshot import` removes every SCIP symbol and occurrence no indexed file owns, with the graph edges at those symbols, because such a row records only a hash of its path and the importing repository's policy cannot be checked against it; the import reports the count as a caveat and a `scip` quality note and lowers `quality.scip_symbols`, `scip_occurrences` and `scip_exact_references`. An imported index therefore no longer resolves references into generated or ignored code until `ok index` runs. An indexed file's references to a withheld or removed symbol stay in its occurrences and still count in `scip_exact_references` and as proven in `resolution_quality`, as references to external dependencies already do; they name no symbol. SCIP's `project_root` is not read, so an index generated in a subdirectory is judged against subdirectory-relative paths and a root-anchored `[paths] deny` glob may miss it. An existing index keeps any such SCIP rows, and still reports itself compatible, until it is rebuilt: `ok watch` does not re-import SCIP, so re-run `ok index` to remove them (#536).
- Cross-project indexing records `IndexQuality.excluded_test_targets` as unrecorded (`None`, omitted from JSON) rather than `{}`: it reads no source, so it examined no test and cannot claim none was excluded. `ok setup audit` on such an index says cross-project mode indexes no tests and points to each linked project's own index, instead of advising a re-index that would record nothing more (#533).
- `ok index` (and `ok watch`, which re-reads history through the same path) no longer stores git history naming a path discovery never reads. A file touch on a secret-like path (`.env`, `*.pem`, `.ssh/`, ...) or a `[paths] deny` path, a touch renamed from one, its patch, and every co-change pair naming it are dropped before anything is written, so `git_file_touches`, `git_cochange_edges`, churn hotspots and the history-backed ranking signals cannot surface the name. Dropping, rather than storing a redacted name, matches discovery (which never indexes such a file whatever `redact_secrets` says) and the rows snapshot import purges. The index records how many touches and co-change pairs it withheld, without naming them, in a `git_history` quality note (`quality.quality_notes` in `ok --json status` and MCP `repo_status`), and a skipped commit patch whose reason quotes such a path has it masked. An existing index keeps any such rows, and still reports itself compatible, until it is rebuilt: re-run `ok index` to remove them. Co-change is still computed over every path a commit touched, so a commit above `max_files_per_commit` stays excluded and the pairs between the other files it touched are unchanged. A file merely named for credentials, such as `config/credentials.json`, is indexed (redacted) and keeps its history. Commit messages are stored as written (#525).
- A Rust `crate::` call path is read against the caller's own crate. The resolver spelled `crate::util::f()` as `src::util::f` whatever file wrote it, so in a Cargo workspace whose root is also a package, a call in member `crates/a` got an `Exact` `CALLS` edge to the root package's `src/util.rs::f` instead of `crates/a/src/util.rs::f`, and `crate::only_root::z()` got one to a module `crates/a` does not have, which rustc rejects; in a virtual workspace, where no top-level `src/` exists, every `crate::` call path in a member resolved to nothing. The module tree now records, for each Rust file of a crate module tree, the tree and the crate roots whose tree holds it, and `crate::`, `self::` and `super::` paths are spelled from that crate and end only in a file placed in it. A package's crate module trees are its library and default binary in `src/` (`lib.rs`, `main.rs`) and the targets Cargo discovers: a file directly in `src/bin/`, `tests/`, `examples/` or `benches/` is a crate root whose modules live beside it in that directory, as for any crate root file, and `<dir>/<name>/main.rs` is one whose modules live in `<dir>/<name>/`. So `crate::` in a member names the member's modules, `crate::f()` names the item of the root file whose tree holds the caller (`main.rs` for a module only `main.rs` declares, not the library's `lib.rs`), a `super::` climb past the crate root proves nothing, and `crate::x::f()` in `src/bin/tool.rs` names `src/bin/x.rs` or `src/bin/x/mod.rs`, not the library's `src/x.rs` it reached before; the previous release also read `src/bin/tool/sub.rs` as `tool`'s `mod sub;`, which rustc looks for in `src/bin/`. `mod common;` in an integration test `tests/it.rs` now reaches `tests/common/mod.rs` for call paths and `use` binding, where both were left unresolved, and `use crate::...` and `use super::...` in `src/bin/`, `tests/`, `examples/` and `benches/` files bind through the declared module tree like those of `src/`. A file of `src/` that the declared module tree does not place at its path, such as a module declared inside a macro, still reads `crate::` against the indexed `lib.rs` and `main.rs`, as before; one in `src/bin/`, `tests/`, `examples/` or `benches/`, such as a helper an integration test mounts with `#[path]`, is read against no crate, since each root there is an independent crate, so its module paths are left unresolved. A raw-identifier module (`pub mod r#type;`) is the file `type.rs`: it was recorded under `r#type`, so the file was treated as unplaced and neither `crate::r#type::ty()` nor `use crate::r#type::...` reached it. A `[lib] path` naming a file directly in the package's `src/` (`[lib] path = "src/app_lib.rs"`) is now that crate's root, where every module path in the crate was left unresolved; the manifest is read for `[lib] path` only, not `[[bin]] path`. A package with indexed files in a crate module tree whose crate root the index cannot place (for example a `lib.rs` or `main.rs` discovery did not index, such as one over `max_file_size`, a `[lib] path` outside `src/`, or a crate whose only root is a `[[bin]] path` target) no longer loses its module-path edges silently: the index records a `relationship_resolution` quality note with the number of such packages, naming none, in `quality.quality_notes` (`ok --json status`, MCP `repo_status`). On this repository's own index (a virtual workspace) 38 exact `CALLS` edges are added, all `crate::` calls inside a member crate, and none is lost or retargeted; no other edge changes. On a large async-runtime crate (555 Rust files) no edge changes. `RELATIONSHIP_RESOLVER_SEMANTICS_VERSION` is now `ri3-relationship-resolver-v8`, so an existing index reports `RebuildRequired` and must be rebuilt with `ok index`. `open_kioku_resolution::ScopeIndex::record_misplaced_rust_module_files` (from #535, unreleased) is replaced by `record_rust_module_placements` with the new `RustModulePlacement`; with nothing recorded, no Rust module path spelled from file paths is followed. Nine Rust `CALLS` conformance cases cover the workspace shapes, `src/bin/` and integration-test crate roots, a `#[path]` helper shared by integration tests, a raw-identifier module and a `[lib] path` crate root (compact count 96) (#542, #543).
- A Rust call through a `crate::`, `self::` or `super::` path no longer proves an `Exact` `CALLS` edge into a file that a `#[path]` attribute keeps out of the module the path spells. With `#[path = "elsewhere.rs"] mod pathed;` in `src/w.rs` and a stale `src/w/pathed.rs` at the default location, `self::pathed::p()` in `w.rs`, `super::pathed::p()` in `src/w/child.rs` and `crate::w::pathed::p()` each got an exact edge to `src/w/pathed.rs::p`, which rustc never compiles; so did `self::inner::deep::d()` into the default location of `#[path]` declared inside an inline `mod inner`, and `super::target_fn()` in a file mounted by `#[path]`, which climbed the directories of its own file path to `legacy/mod.rs` instead of its real parent module. The resolver spells these paths from file paths, and now neither starts nor ends one in a file of a crate's `src/` tree that the declared module tree does not place where its path says, the rule Rust `use` binding already follows: a file is placed when it is a crate root, or when every module from the crate root down is declared with `mod name;` (no body, no `#[path]`) by the module above it, and a crate with no `Cargo.toml` is read against a top-level `src/`. So a path into or out of the default location of a `#[path]` module, a file a `#[path]` mounts, a file declared inside an inline `mod` block, or a file no `mod` declares is left unresolved rather than followed to the attribute's file, and such a call loses its edge even where the layout happened to match. That includes modules declared inside a macro (`cfg_if! { mod unix; }`, `cfg_*! { pub mod runtime; }`), whose `mod` the declared tree does not see, and every file of a crate whose root is set by `[lib] path` or `[[bin]] path` or whose `lib.rs`/`main.rs` is not indexed (for example, larger than `max_file_size`): there every module-path `CALLS` edge in the crate is left unresolved. On a large async-runtime workspace (555 Rust files, most modules declared inside `cfg_*!` macros) 14 of 1514 exact `CALLS` edges were lost this way and none was added or retargeted. Files the tree cannot place either way (under `src/bin/`, `tests/`, `examples/`, or a `src/` below a directory with no `Cargo.toml`) keep the previous layout-only reading, so a `#[path]` decoy there is not covered; paths within one file, through its own inline `mod` blocks, are unchanged. On this repository's own index no `CALLS` edge, and no edge of any other type, is added, lost or retargeted, and the retrieval baseline and workflow benchmark are unchanged. `RELATIONSHIP_RESOLVER_SEMANTICS_VERSION` is now `ri3-relationship-resolver-v7`, so an existing index reports `RebuildRequired` and must be rebuilt with `ok index` to drop the edges. `open_kioku_resolution::ScopeIndex` gains `record_misplaced_rust_module_files`; with nothing recorded every file keeps the previous layout-only reading. Three `MustNotEmit` Rust `CALLS` conformance cases cover the decoy and the mounted file (compact count 87) (#535).
- The symbol-registry pass no longer links a Rust bare name to an item of its file that module scoping keeps out of reach. In `fn now() {}` followed by `#[cfg(test)] mod tests { use mock_clock::now; #[test] fn t() { now() } }`, the call in `t` got a High-confidence `open-kioku-symbol-registry/same-file` `CALLS` edge to the file's `now`, which MCP `get_references` with `kind: "callers"` listed as a caller and `ok impact` reported under `possible_impact`. The registry now consults the resolver's rule from #484 (an item of the file is in reach only from its own module or one that arrives there through `use super::*` globs, and not while an explicit import of the name to another path is in scope), but drops a candidate only when that verdict rests on imports the rule models exactly: a resolved target, a one-item `self::x`/`super::x` path to a module that declares `x` itself and imports no `x` and no glob of this crate, a `crate::` path or an external crate, with no glob of this crate other than a `use super::*` chain on the way. An import it cannot place, such as `use super::helpers::make;`, `use super::x;` where the parent has `x` through its own `use` or glob, `use self::inner::f;`, `pub use inner::f;`, `use self::inner::*;`, `use crate::this_file::*;`, `use crate::*;` or a glob inside a function body, keeps the candidate, so a valid call does not lose its caller edge. The check covers every registry strategy, because the registry's imports are file-wide and an import resolving to this file would otherwise offer the item again through `direct-import`. Ruling out some of a strategy's candidates leaves the name ambiguous as before rather than making the rest the answer, and a member use of the name on the same line (`let path = dir.path();`) does not take the place of the ruled-out item; the trade-off is that such a member use loses its edge even when that item is its real target, as in `let value = s.value();` inside a `mod tests` where the file's `S::value` is out of reach of the bare `value` but is what `s.value()` calls. Only bare Rust names are scoped: `super::now()`, `x.now()` and the name a `mod` item declares match as before, and other languages are unchanged. On this repository's own index the change removes 606 registry edges (597 `REFERENCES`, 9 `CALLS`; 466 point at an item declared inside a `mod` block, mostly `tests` helpers matched from production code, and the rest are tokens in a `mod tests` that imports named items only, matched to file-level methods) and adds none; no resolver, SCIP, import or other edge class changes, and the retrieval baseline and workflow benchmark are unchanged. `get_references` callers and impact's `possible_impact` lose these edges on Rust repositories that use inline `mod` blocks. `RELATIONSHIP_RESOLVER_SEMANTICS_VERSION` is now `ri3-relationship-resolver-v6`, so an existing index reports `RebuildRequired` until `ok index`. A relationship conformance case can set `forbid_heuristic_edges`, which makes a non-authoritative edge from its source a `must_not_emit_heuristic_violation`; two Rust `CALLS` cases use it (compact count 84) (#526).
- Context packs, plans and every surface built on them rank a file that matches the whole task in code spelling with the task's other whole-task hits. A task word with a code-vocabulary alias (`configuration` → `config`, `defaults` → `default`, `histories` → `history`) used to be searched in that spelling only word by word, after every literal term, so for "add history configuration defaults" the file defining `HistoryConfig` and its default functions was lexical rank 48 and last in the plan by a 3e-4 margin; the whole task is now also searched in code spelling and the two hit lists share the first tier by score, putting that file first. Literal whole-task hits the shared tier displaces are kept right after it, never dropped. Tasks with no aliased word are unaffected. On the commit-derived corpora Java, Go and Python gain recall at 10–20 and TypeScript is identical, but Python's holdout loses one rank-1 hit (R@1 0.4422 → 0.4372, MRR 0.5426 → 0.5397); every gated family stays within tolerance.
- A plan whose validation selection exceeds its cap of eight targets says so. `ok plan` used to drop the extra plausible targets without a record, and `ok verify`, which recommends every plausible target, then reported each one as `recommended test <name> is not in the saved plan`, indistinguishable from a test the plan should have listed and missed. The plan now records how many targets the cap alone dropped as `validation_omitted` and their ids as `validation_omitted_ids` (both omitted from JSON when zero), and adds the line `validation selection capped at 8 targets; N further plausible targets were not planned` to `risk.reasons` (so it also reaches a change contract's risk reasons) and a matching `recommended_next_steps` entry. Neither changes the plan's confidence or risk score: a change with many tests is not missing evidence. Targets removed earlier by the plausibility predicate or by the per-file preference for a suite name are not counted. `ok verify` still reports every unplanned recommendation as a `missing_test` finding and its verdict is unchanged; for a target the saved plan disclosed as capped, the reason now reads `recommended test <name> is not in the saved plan: the plan's validation cap omitted it`. A plan under the cap gains neither field nor risk reason. A change contract carries the reason but not the ids, so verifying against a contract keeps the plain wording (#496).
- `ok tests`, MCP `find_tests_for_change` and `ok setup audit` tell "every test here is skipped" from "there are no tests". The selector withheld targets the runner skips (`test.skip`, `it.todo`, `test.failing`) without saying so, so a file whose tests were all skipped returned the same empty list as a file with none, and the setup audit advised `Index test files before relying on validation recommendations.` for a repository whose test files were indexed. The selection now counts what it withheld by reason in `excluded`, names up to five in `excluded_sample`, and whenever `tests` is empty carries a caveat saying either that no indexed test target was found for the path or that N were found and all excluded, with the step that would make them count; the withheld targets are never listed in `tests`, and what is recommended is unchanged. The index records the same count as `quality.excluded_test_targets` in `ok --json status` and `repo_status`, and the audit's `tests` and `validation` checks report it, using the context pack's wording (`every indexed test target is a disabled test the runner skips`), with the next step `Enable the skipped tests (…) before relying on validation recommendations.` An index written before this change has no such count; for one with no runnable test target the audit says so and advises re-indexing to tell skipped tests from absent ones. MCP `find_tests_for_change` no longer describes itself as returning repository-wide test evidence when `path` is omitted, which it never did: it selects nothing, and now says so in a caveat (refs #530). With `limit: 0` the caveat says how many runnable targets matched instead of claiming none exist. `ok tests` also prints its targets, withheld counts and caveats as text when the JSON is too long to print, where it printed nothing (#494).
- Context packs, plans and preflight reports pair each primary and supporting result's `evidence_refs` one to one with its `evidence` lines: the i-th ref names the fact the i-th line states, and no ref appears twice on a result. Stream deduplication sorted the lines and the refs separately, fusion appended other streams' refs to the chosen unit without their lines, and region widening and runtime and history annotation appended lines and refs independently, so a result could list 9 lines and 5 refs, a ref could name another line's fact, and most lines had no ref. Retrieval refs on a primary result, and on its entry in `retrieval_diagnostics.selection.selected_units`, are now the `search:<path>:<range>:<index>` ids of the pack records carrying the same line, so each resolves to exactly that record; refs from other producers keep their ids (graph edge, symbol, document, region step, runtime signal, history fact). A graph-neighbor line cites its first incident edge and a history-signal line its component's first fact, with every edge and fact still listed on the score component; a line whose fact the result already cites is not repeated. `region:adjacent-unit` refs now carry the path (`region:adjacent-unit:src/main.rs:1-4`), since a range alone was ambiguous across files. History-signal reasons are now sorted together with their score components (`open-kioku-storage`), so a history line and its record cite the component the reason describes; sorted alone, `similar change overlap: 2 similar historical change(s)` moved ahead of `… 3 persisted co-change neighbor(s)` and each line cited the other signal's facts. Plan evidence caps (10 per boundary rule, 3 borrowed, 50 on the boundary) now choose what to keep by authority - refs of exact-reference results first, then exact `symbol:` anchors, then direct graph edges (`edge:`), then lexical refs - and list the kept refs in file order as before; with one ref per line a result's lexical `search:` refs could otherwise fill a rule's ten slots and cut its `symbol:` anchor. Evidence lines, pack records, confidence and ranking are unchanged: `evidence_density` still counts one retrieval fact per primary result, and the retrieval and workflow benchmarks are identical. A result whose refs still fail to pair when the pack is built has them replaced with positional ids and a retrieval caveat names it (#433).
- `open_kioku_git::commit` resolves `HEAD` through Git when the branch ref is packed (after `git gc` or in a fresh clone) or the checkout is a linked worktree, where it returned `None`; manifests record the commit in those checkouts, and `ok snapshot export` no longer writes `repo_commit: "unknown"` for them (#473).
- `ok watch` removes the analysis facts other files hold about a file it deletes, such as a git co-change with it (facts whose target is that file; a fact that merely spells the same string, such as an unresolved import specifier, is kept), as a full index never records a fact about a file it does not hold. They survived an incremental update, kept serving co-change evidence that named the deleted path, and made a snapshot of that index fail import's consistency check (#473).
- `ok watch` writes graph-node documents into the search index, as `ok index` does. It rebuilt the Tantivy index from code chunks only, on its initial index and on every change after it, so once a watch process had written the index `ok search --kind graph` and MCP `search_code` with `mode: "graph"` returned no results, with no warning, until the next full `ok index`. An incremental update adds the documents from the graph it just stored, a full rebuild from the graph it just replaced. As hardening, an incremental update over a store whose graph is marked for a rebuild (edges discarded when an older index format was opened) now escalates to a full rebuild, which clears the mark, rather than leaving it set; `ok watch` itself starts with a full index, so it rarely reaches that state (#498).
- `ok impact` and MCP `impact_analysis` rank exact references above every heuristic direct impact (git co-change, runtime, service boundary, lexical) before they apply the 25-entry cap, and order by score only within those two tiers; an equal score then breaks by kind (exact reference, co-change, runtime, service boundary, lexical) before path. Scores are on different scales per kind (an exact reference is `1.25 +` occurrence confidence, a lexical hit is raw BM25 plus boosts), so keyword matches used to fill the cap and cut proven references; exact references now come first in `direct_impacts`, which also changes which five direct impacts seed the indirect search, and which files context packs and `plan_change` show: `supporting_files` (the first 10 direct impacts) and `recommended_change_boundary.caution_files` (the first 8) now list exact references ahead of higher-scoring heuristic impacts. The report now counts what the caps cut, in `direct_impacts_omitted` and `indirect_impacts_omitted` and in a `risk_report.reasons` line that says how many of the cut entries were exact references (non-zero only when exact references alone exceed the cap), and the text output prints the count under the list; previously the cut was silent. An indirect dependent found at two scores is listed once: the path deduplication only folded adjacent entries, so the same file could fill two of the 15 indirect slots. Which chunks a grouped direct impact lists no longer relies on an earlier sort to be independent of arrival order (#497, refs #468).
- The git history patch scan that feeds per-symbol history touches (`commit_patches`, read by indexing's symbol-touch mapping) ends each hunk where its header counts say, so an added source line beginning `++ ` (rendered `+++ `) is no longer recorded as the commit's changed path, and it pins `--src-prefix=a/ --dst-prefix=b/`, so `diff.noprefix` or custom diff prefixes cannot change the recorded path (with `diff.noprefix`, a file under a top-level `b/` directory lost that directory). A record-separator byte inside a text diff no longer splits a commit's patch in two, because a record now has to start a line. A commit whose patch cannot be read is skipped, not fatal: indexing continues, and a `git_history` quality note gives the count of skipped commits and the first one with its reason. Commit history and co-change edges come from `--name-status` and were not affected (#482).
- A `---`/`+++` path holding a space no longer keeps the tab git appends after it, so the history scan and `diff --unified=0` parsing record `src/sp ace.rs` rather than `src/sp ace.rs\t`, and those files' symbol touches and changed ranges are no longer missed. The verify parser already stripped it; both now share one rule. Verify also keeps a spaced `---`/`+++` path that has no TAB whole instead of cutting it at the first space, and drops trailing spaces from it; git always appends a TAB after such a name, so this only affects hand-written or non-git diffs.
- A plan that cannot become a change contract (no stable evidence references, or no primary context and no allowed files) is still verified directly, but `check_api_surface` and `check_dependency_delta` are no longer silently ignored there: each one enabled adds an `api_surface_check_not_run` or `dependency_delta_check_not_run` warning giving the reason, and the verdict is at least `warn`. A repository with an architecture policy enables the dependency check, so such a plan now warns there too (#486).
- MCP `verify_change` with a `plan` accepts `check_deps` and `changed` as the contract path already did; `check_deps: true` used to be dropped, so the dependency check did not run (#486).
- A Rust call or receiver type inside an inline `mod` block no longer proves an edge to a same-named item elsewhere in its file. In `fn now() {}` followed by `mod tests { use mock_clock::now; fn t() { now() } }`, the call in `t` used to get an authoritative `CALLS` edge (strategy `lexical_scope`) to the file's `now`, which Rust does not put in scope there; and with `mod fakes { pub struct Client; impl Client { .. } }` beside `use reqwest::Client;`, `Client::new()` and `c.get()` in a file-level function were proven against `fakes::Client`. Lexical lookup of a Rust name now stops at the enclosing `mod` block, at a scope that imports the name, and at a scope with a glob import; `use super::name;`, `use self::name;` (with or without an alias) looks the name up in the module the path names, and so does a `mod` block whose only globs are the same `use super::*` path; beside another glob such as `use proptest::prelude::*;`, `use super::*` still reaches an item the parent module declares itself, since a second glob supplying the same name would not compile. A glob inside a function body still stops the lookup, because it may shadow the module's item. A same-file type is a receiver or declared-type candidate only when that lookup reaches it, so a type in a sibling `mod` block never is, and a nearer import of the name decides instead. Associated functions of an `impl` or `trait` are no longer lexical candidates for a bare call: in a method `fn reconcile(&mut self) { reconcile(..) }` the call used to be proven as a call of the method itself rather than of the free function it names. Calls to items of the same module, through `use super::*`, and through a `use` inside the block still resolve. The resolver's non-authoritative same-file fallback (`same_file_candidate`) no longer offers a Rust item that scoping rules out: one in a module the call neither is in nor reaches through `use super::*`, or any item while an explicit import of the name to another path is in scope. That candidate is counted in the resolution quality report (`heuristic candidates retained`) and is not written to the graph; the High-confidence `same-file` `CALLS` edge that `get_references` `kind: callers` and `ok impact` `possible_impact` show for such a call comes from the symbol-registry pass, which this change does not touch. `RELATIONSHIP_RESOLVER_SEMANTICS_VERSION` is now `ri3-relationship-resolver-v4`, so an existing index reports `RebuildRequired` and must be rebuilt with `ok index`. Relationship conformance adds ten Rust `CALLS` cases (compact count 73) and stays at zero diagnostics, false positives and false negatives (#484).
- Two fresh indexes of the same tree, built by the same binary on the same platform, now give bit-identical lexical scores. Tantivy's default writer split documents across its indexing threads, so segment layout and document ids changed from run to run. The pruning scorer sums a document's per-term BM25 contributions in an order that depends on that layout, so `bm25_relevance`, and every `text_relevance`, fused and impact score derived from it, could differ by one or two ULP (up to 1.5e-5 measured on this repository). That was enough for near-tied candidates to compare unequal and skip the equal-score tie-breaks, which moved one case between rank 7 and 8 across two runs of the same commit on the TypeScript corpus (~900 files). The search index is now written by one indexing thread, in repository order (path, line range, id), with background merges disabled, and any segments flushed past the memory budget are merged once, in that order, into a single segment. A document's id is therefore its repository-order rank, whatever order the caller passed. The guarantee is per platform and toolchain: BM25's idf uses `f32::ln`, which goes to the platform math library, so scores can still differ between macOS and Linux. Measured on this repository (400 files): two fresh `ok index` runs produce byte-identical `ok --json search` output for all 30 retrieval-benchmark queries, against 237 differing values on the previous build. Two hosted commit-derived runs of this change produce identical reports on all four corpora; two runs of one earlier main commit had differed in 92 fields on the TypeScript corpus. Against main, one Python-corpus dev case gained a rank (MRR 0.49014 to 0.49050) on a corpus whose main runs were already identical to each other: a near-tie now broken one fixed way, not a better ranking. One TypeScript-corpus dev field moved the other way: gold line-yield 0.3763 to 0.3746, just below the 0.3752–0.3765 seen across three main runs, likewise a near-tie broken one fixed way. **Indexing is slower on large repositories.** The hosted "Index the corpus" step (all of `ok index`) took 254–266 s over 2 runs on the Java service (~10k files) against 208–256 s over 4 main runs, about +8% by mean, or +11% by mean (+7% by median) against only the three main runs at the two most recent commits (the lower main bound is one outlier), and 66–68 s against 59–63 s on the Python ML library (~4k files), non-overlapping and +5% to +15%. The main runs were at commits `2dd4dc84`, `3db593c0` (two runs) and `2b629fbe`; the branch also carries later main commits. Smaller corpora were unchanged (8–11 s). The hosted logs carry no per-stage timings, so the search-index stage cost on large corpora is unmeasured; since that stage is roughly 15% of `ok index` on this repository, an increase of this size in the whole command suggests the stage itself slowed by tens of percent there (an inference, not a measurement). On this repository the stage was not slower: a median of 2.4 s against 3.8 s over ten alternating runs (ranges 1.8–5.5 s and 2.9–17.2 s). Under heavy disk contention the single writer degraded more than the multi-threaded one (2–37 s against 2.7–18 s over eight rounds), because document-store compression and writes now run on one thread. The indexing memory budget is unchanged at 50 MB, but merge-time peak memory was not measured. When more than one segment is flushed, the final merge writes the merged segment before deleting the old ones, so the search index's disk use briefly roughly doubles (an estimated peak of about 1.1 GB on a ~16k-file Java index). Tantivy 0.26 has no index sorting, so documents cannot be written by several threads and then put in a deterministic order afterwards (#491, refs #468).
- A Rust call through a `self::` or `super::` path inside an inline `mod` block resolves from that block rather than from the file's module path. In `src/worker.rs`, `fn f() {}` followed by `mod tests { fn t() { super::f() } }` used to climb from the file path to the crate root, so the call lost its edge to `worker::f`, and got an authoritative `CALLS` edge to the crate root's `f` whenever `src/lib.rs` declared one; `self::f()` inside the block likewise proved an edge to any `f` of the file, including one the block's own `use` shadows. Each `super` now leaves the innermost module around the call, an inline `mod` block counting as one, so `super::super::f()` from `mod tests` names the crate root's `f`, and nested blocks climb one at a time; a path segment naming an inline `mod` block descends into it (`self::outer::inner::g()`). A path that lands on a module of the file names an item that module declares, or one it brings in from another module of the file through `use super::name;`, `use self::name;` or `use super::*`, so the same-named items of its `impl` blocks and nested `mod` blocks, which share its qualified name, are no longer candidates, and an item the module imports from anywhere else gets no authoritative edge. A path through `mod name;` continues into that module's file, and a path that leaves the file keeps the file-path rule, now also for more than one `super`, so `super::child::f()` in the crate root's `mod tests` now resolves into `src/child.rs`. These edges carry the strategies `rust_module_scope_path` and `rust_module_scope_member` instead of `rust_crate_qualified_module` and `rust_crate_qualified_member`. `RELATIONSHIP_RESOLVER_SEMANTICS_VERSION` is now `ri3-relationship-resolver-v5`, so an existing index reports `RebuildRequired` and must be rebuilt with `ok index`. Relationship conformance adds nine Rust `CALLS` cases (compact count 82) and stays at zero diagnostics, false positives and false negatives (#520).
- A Rust `use` path inside the importing file's own crate resolves through that crate's declared module tree, so its `IMPORTS` edge names the file declaring the module or the item. `use crate::auth::issue_token;`, `use crate::auth::Token;` and `use crate::auth::*;` in `src/session.rs` each reach `src/auth.rs`, where all three used to merge into one High-confidence edge to `src/lib.rs` carrying three `import_binding` proofs, so `ok impact --file src/auth.rs` now lists `src/session.rs` as an `IMPORTS` dependent and `ok impact --file src/lib.rs` no longer does. `crate::` starts at the `src/` of the nearest `Cargo.toml`, `self::`/`super::` at the importing module, and a path spelled with the package's own crate name (`demo_crate::auth`) at that package's `lib.rs`, so an integration test under `tests/` resolves as well; every module on the path must be declared as a file (`mod name;` with no body and no `#[path]`), and a relative path written inside an inline `mod` block resolves to the file it is written in when it stops at the module the hops land on (`mod tests { use super::*; }` names that file's own module, so no edge is emitted and no import is counted unresolved), while one naming something below that module (`use super::helpers::Thing;`) or climbing above the file (`use super::super::*;`) stays unresolved so the absence is reported. A glob reaches the globbed module's file, an item the file of the module declaring it, and the crate root is a target only when the item is declared there — an item reachable only through a `pub use` re-export stays unresolved. The `rust-crate-root` fallback is gone; the strategies are `rust-module-path` and `rust-item-module`, and a path the module tree cannot answer is `Unresolved` with Low confidence, a caveat and no binding proof instead of a proven edge into the crate root. A Rust module is `x.rs` or `x/mod.rs` and never a file inside `x/`, so `use crate::auth;` is no longer ambiguous where `src/auth.rs` and `src/auth/keys.rs` both exist. The static `use`-syntax edge no longer asserts a module for an import the resolver answered: it reads the resolver's own `IMPORTS` fact for the row rather than re-deriving "in-crate" from the path text, which could not tell `my_crate::auth` from an external path and left that spelling asserting a module beside the file the resolver had just proved. Where the resolver abstained, its fact edge shares the syntax edge's target node and merging unions proofs, so this also stops a syntax binding upgrading a recorded absence into an authoritative edge whose message reads `unresolved import`. A builtin or external package produces a `DEPENDS_ON` fact instead, so `std::fmt` keeps its module binding and stays the only record of that import. `RELATIONSHIP_RESOLVER_SEMANTICS_VERSION` is now `ri3-relationship-resolver-v3` and `GRAPH_EMISSION_SEMANTICS_VERSION` is now `ri3-graph-emission-v2`, so an index built before this change reports `RebuildRequired` until `ok index` rebuilds it (#458).
- Exact-reference provenance on impact results is typed. A result produced from an indexed symbol occurrence carries `exact_reference_provenance` (`scip`, `tree_sitter` or `lsp`), and deduplication keeps it when a higher-scoring lexical hit on the same path and range is merged in. The `exact symbol reference via` prefix of `match_reason` used to be the only marker, so that merge erased it and the reference silently left `exact_reference_count`. Context and plan exact-reference counts, impact's direct-impact classification, and ranking's `exact_reference` signal read the field; ranking's substring fallback on "exact symbol reference" is removed. The `impact:<path>` evidence record takes the source type of its exact references (SCIP, then LSP, then tree-sitter) and names every source in its message, instead of `scip` whenever any exact reference existed, and exact-reference counts accept `scip`, `tree_sitter` and `lsp` evidence explicitly. An occurrence from a lexical or heuristic source is no longer reported as an exact reference "via indexed". Missing-exact-reference negative evidence lists `impact.exact_reference_provenance` as the inspected source (#438).
- Impact deduplication keeps each evidence line with the ref it was published with. Refs that do not align one-to-one with a result's lines are replaced by per-line derived `search:` ids instead of being paired by position, which gave a runtime line from a second fact a lexical id and left that fact uncited; a line whose id is already cited under other text takes the next unused derived id. A grouped direct impact names every non-representative chunk in its listed lines or its "more matching ranges" summary, including a chunk whose lines are all already cited (#463).
- A bare call through a Rust item import (`use crate::auth::issue_token;`, one path of `use crate::auth::{issue_token, Token};`, or `use crate::auth::issue_token as mint;`) is an authoritative `CALLS` edge with the `exact_call_site`, `import_binding` and `qualified_name` proofs and resolver strategy `rust_item_import`, so `ok impact` and `impact_analysis` report it in `proven_impact`; it used to produce no edge and no impact. Rust imports are bound only by following the path through the importing file's own crate: `crate::` starts at the `src/` of the nearest `Cargo.toml` and `self::`/`super::` at the importing module, which must itself be declared where its path says; every module on the path must be declared as a file (`mod name;` with no body and no `#[path]`); and exactly one module-level Rust item of that name must be defined there. In a package with both `src/lib.rs` and `src/main.rs`, `crate::` follows only the root whose module tree declares the importing file. A path naming a declared module binds that module's file, and a path naming both a submodule and an item binds no call target. The module-key lookup other languages use no longer binds Rust imports, so `crate::auth::issue_token` in one workspace member cannot bind to another member's `auth/issue_token.rs`. For Rust only, a call through an import is a `CALLS` edge only when the bound item is a function; other languages keep their existing import-bound call targets. A declared type reached through an import also carries an `import_binding` proof. An associated-function call through an imported type (`use crate::auth::Token;` then `Token::parse(raw)`) resolves to that function as an authoritative `CALLS` edge. A receiver whose type comes from an initializer is proven only by a constructor form: `Foo { .. }`, a Rust path call `Foo::bar(..)` whose indexed signature returns `Self` or `Foo`, or `Foo::default()` on a struct, enum or alias with no indexed `default` (a derived `Default`). `let handle = Server::spawn()` returning a `ServerHandle` no longer types `handle` as `Server`. A `self.field` receiver matched through a local binding of the same name gives candidates without the receiver-type proof. A Rust parameter or `let` type that names a type parameter of the enclosing function, impl or trait (`fn f<Token: Parse>(t: Token)`) is no longer recorded as that type. Grouped Rust imports are recorded as one import per path, deduplicated per path and line, so `use a::{B, B as C};` stores one row and keeps both bindings. `PARSER_SEMANTICS_VERSION` is now `tier1-parser-semantics-v3` and `RELATIONSHIP_RESOLVER_SEMANTICS_VERSION` is now `ri3-relationship-resolver-v2`, so an index built before this change reports `RebuildRequired` until `ok index` rebuilds it (#453).
- `ok verify` text and HTML reports list the changed lines no symbol covers (`<path>:<start>-<end>`, the same entries as `changed_regions_without_symbol` in JSON) under "Changed regions without a symbol", so a comment-only edit outside every function no longer shows nothing. The `symbol_granularity` warning now says "the supplied diff has no hunk ranges for this path" for a binary or mode-only entry of a supplied diff, and keeps "no diff was supplied for this path" for paths given only with `--changed`. A hunk that replaces a whole `impl` or class lists that container beside its methods instead of only the methods (#464).
- A plan's caution and allowed rules no longer each copy a whole evidence-ref list. A rule cites at most ten refs from its own path's impact or primary-context entries, in file order (path, then numeric line range, then evidence line, so line 2 comes before line 10; the plan's 50-ref `recommended_change_boundary.evidence_refs` and every `evidence_by_section` list use the same order, so each cap keeps a path's first lines); a rule whose path has no evidence of its own (a caution file the context boundary names but the impact report does not hold) cites three refs of the plan's `recommended_change_boundary.evidence_refs`, which is capped at 50, instead of the context boundary's uncapped list. The refs a cap leaves out are counted in a new `evidence_refs_omitted` field (omitted from JSON when zero, shown as "+N more not listed" in text, Markdown and HTML, and as its own column in TOON), and every ref a plan derives for a rule is listed in `evidence_by_section`. `plan_change` payloads no longer grow with the number of caution files times the length of the context ref list (#462).
- Indexing the same tree twice with the same build gives the same context, search and impact ordering (evidence-ref selection for one impact entry remains unstable; see #468). Tantivy broke equal search scores by the segment a document landed in, which depends on how its indexing threads split the work, and rank fusion turned that tie order into different context scores: two `workflow-bench` runs of one build, each re-indexing this repository, swapped the context paths at positions 6 and 7 for the `git-history` and `parse-java` cases. Search documents now carry their position in repository order (path, line range, id) and equal scores are broken on it. Discovery processes files in path order instead of the order the filesystem lists them. The search, reranking, context and candidate-stream sorts break any remaining tie on path and then line range, and chunk, test and analysis-fact reads order their ties by id instead of row order. Exact-flat semantic search orders equally similar vectors of one target by vector id instead of hash order. Impact results are ordered by score, then path, line range and evidence ids before the report truncates them, so which files a report keeps no longer depends on the order its streams produced them in. Graph nodes reach the search index in node-id order, git symbol touches that share a qualified name are ordered by symbol id, and the manifest, runtime and validation-report walks visit entries in name order, so what their caps keep no longer depends on filesystem listing order. Graph neighbor reads order edges by id before their limit, so a node with more edges than the limit keeps the same edges after an `ok watch` update as after a fresh index. Unequal scores are ordered as before. The rank is stored in a new `order_key` fast field of the Tantivy search index. A search index written before this change has no `order_key`: it still opens and searches, `ok doctor` and `ok status` do not report it as damaged, and the analysis-semantics status is unchanged, but ties in it keep the old order until the next `ok index` rebuilds it (#468).
- The symbol registry reports the same `symbol_registry_unresolved` quality notes on every run of the same build. Its cap of 64 notes was shared by parallel workers, so whichever chunks finished first claimed it and different names appeared from run to run. A name repeated on several lines of one chunk also used a slot per line, so a run reported fewer than 64 distinct notes. The cap is now spent in chunk order on distinct notes, so an index with more unresolved names than the cap reports exactly 64. A symbol reached through two imports of the same file is also counted once in the registry's candidate count (#461).
- `ok watch` replaces exactly the changed file's graph edges on an incremental re-index and reconciles the rest of the stored graph with the new snapshot by identity, so a renamed symbol loses its old callers, edges from unchanged files into nodes the change removed go with them, and the graph string dictionary does not grow across repeated runs. The per-file delete used to key on the producing pass name, which never matched a path, so no edge was ever removed (#413).
- `ok index` and `ok watch` publish the index manifest as the last step of a run. A full run removes the manifest when it stages the rows and puts it back after the graph and the Tantivy index are written, so a reader never opens a manifest over rows or a graph from a different run and a run that fails partway leaves the repository unindexed. An incremental `ok watch` run replaces the changed files' rows and graph in one transaction under the previous manifest, then rebuilds the Tantivy index in place, during which `ok search` and `search_code` (`mode=code`) fall back to SQLite chunk search or read an index that is empty until the rebuild commits, and `mode=graph` reports the index missing; if a step after that transaction fails, the run withdraws the previous manifest and returns the error. `.ok/index.lock` is an OS advisory lock (`flock`, `LockFileEx`) held for the whole run and released by the kernel however the writer exits; while a live writer holds it and no manifest is published, `ok status`, `ok doctor`, every read command, and the MCP server report `indexing in progress` instead of `repository is not indexed`, and a lock file with no holder is ignored by readers and taken over by the next writer.
- The MCP server keeps serving when its index probe fails (a live writer holds the lock, the file is unreadable, the manifest is newer than the binary): the request that hit the failure gets a `-32000` error carrying the reason, the handshake and `tools/list` still answer, and the failure is logged once per distinct message. The session used to end without a response. A session that already serves an index probes again when the manifest is withdrawn.
- `IndexManifest.schema_version` is 3. A manifest with a newer version than the binary supports is refused by every read surface and by `ok snapshot import` with `index was written by a newer Open Kioku (...): upgrade Open Kioku or run `ok index` to rebuild it`, instead of a serde error or `repository is not indexed`; older manifests still read.
- `ok snapshot import` holds `.ok/index.lock` for the whole import and publishes the manifest last, as `ok index` does: the imported database is moved into place with its manifest removed, the Tantivy index is rebuilt from it, and the manifest is put back afterwards, so a reader never opens the imported manifest before its search index exists and an import that fails at the search stage leaves the repository unindexed. The import took no lock before, so it could run alongside `ok index` or `ok watch` on the same repository. The manifest of the database being replaced is withdrawn before that database is moved aside, so a running MCP session switches to the imported index instead of answering from the replaced file. A replaced file that cannot be read as an index for any reason other than not being a SQLite database or lacking a `manifests` table fails the import with the index left in place. If the imported database cannot be moved into place or opened, the previous database is moved back and its manifest restored, only once it is back at the index path; if moving it back fails the error names the backup file that still holds it, and if restoring the manifest fails the error says so, the repository reading as not indexed until `ok index` runs in both cases. An import killed while the previous database is moved aside leaves it at `.ok/.index.sqlite.<pid>.<time>.backup`, which the `repository is not indexed` message then names. `ok snapshot export` opens the index as every read surface does and refuses with `indexing in progress` or `repository is not indexed` instead of `index database is missing` or `snapshot source database has no index manifest` (#446).
- `ok snapshot export --quality fast` copies the index with SQLite's online backup API in one step, under one read transaction, instead of copying the database file's bytes after a best-effort checkpoint. A writer (`ok index`, `ok watch`, an automatic checkpoint) committing during the export could leave the copy without commits still in the WAL or with pages from different committed states, some of which `integrity_check` does not detect. The copy's header is switched to a rollback journal, so the artifact does not depend on a WAL sidecar. Neither quality checkpoints the live index any more, and both read the manifest from the copied state, so a manifest withdrawn during the copy is refused with `indexing in progress` or `repository is not indexed`. `--quality best` still rebuilds with `VACUUM INTO` at zstd level 9; `fast` still copies pages as they are at level 1, larger and quicker. `docs/storage-model.md` describes the difference (#454).
- `ok --help` and every subcommand `--help` describe the command and each option, with enum values listed; 24 of 38 top-level commands, the global `--json` and `--repo` flags, and the whole option lists of `context`, `plan`, `verify`, `index`, `mcp serve` and `mcp install` had no text. No command was renamed, reordered, added or removed. A unit test walks the clap tree and fails on any command or option without help.
- `ok mcp serve` and every CLI read command (`ok status`, `ok doctor`, `ok search`, `ok context`, `ok impact`, `ok plan`, `ok retrieve-context`, `ok memory search|recent`) no longer create `.ok/index.sqlite`, `.ok/context.sqlite` or `.ok/memory.sqlite` on a repository that has never been indexed; `SqliteStore::open_existing`/`open_repo_index`, `ContextHandleStore::open_repo_existing` and `RepoMemoryStore::open_repo_existing` are the non-creating opens, and `SqliteStore::open` remains the writers'. `repo_status` and `ok --json status` return `{indexed: false, index_path, message, next_step}` for such a repository; the indexed answer gains `indexed: true`; every other tool call and read command fails with the same message, which names `ok index <repo>`. `initialize` and `tools/list` still answer. A database without a manifest is reported as unindexed; the pre-4.0 "rebuild" message is unchanged for a real legacy index. An index built while the server runs is served from the next request.
- `ok setup agent --apply` inspects the client's `open-kioku` entry before indexing: an entry that launches `ok mcp serve` for this repository with at most `--repo`, `--read-only` and `--hide-experimental` is kept unchanged (`[kept] config … existing entry preserved`); any other entry, including one passing `--deny-network=false`, `--approval-required=false` or `--allow-command`, is refused before anything is indexed or written, naming `mcpServers.open-kioku` and the compatible value. `--check` reports `[mismatch]` for a conflicting entry or a foreign guidance file and no longer recommends `--apply` in that state.
- Markdown, text and TOON context packs render the file-limit budget as `no token ceiling; file limit N` instead of the `max_tokens` sentinel; `ContextBudget::has_token_ceiling` is the check, and the JSON field is unchanged.
- `impact_analysis` on a path the index does not hold reports `risk_report.level: "unknown"` and names the path in `risk_report.reasons` and its evidence message; `search_code` rejects a blank `query`; `dependency_path` rejects a `from` or `to` that resolves to no indexed file, symbol or graph node; `retrieve_context` rejects an unknown handle. `ok impact`, `ok search`, `ok path` and `ok retrieve-context` apply the same checks. The `{"value": …}` wrapper is unchanged.
- `ok init` and `ok setup agent --apply` write `ok.toml` with the `[ranking]` f32 weights as their shortest decimal (`graph_proximity = 0.35`; the weights now serialize that way in every output), `[runtime]` reduced to `enabled = false` with the provider fields as comments, and a comment documenting `resolution_mode`'s `legacy`, `shadow` and `v2`. The loader reads the file back as the defaults.
- `ok plan` and `plan_change` count a proven dependent in `impact.proven_impact` toward `exact_reference_count` when it is in a file other than the impact target, authoritative, unambiguous, and a `REFERENCES`, `USES_TYPE`, `CALLS`, `IMPLEMENTS` or `EXTENDS` edge; same-file edges, ambiguous edges, and `IMPORTS` edges still do not count, and `ok context` does not count proven dependents. A plan whose only exact evidence is such an edge no longer reports `exact symbol/reference evidence is unavailable`: `evidence_quality.exact_reference_available` becomes true, the `exact_references` negative evidence item, including the one the plan copies from its context pack, is dropped, `exact_references` scores 1.00 instead of 0.25, and the 0.74 cap for plans without exact evidence no longer applies, so such a plan can read High instead of Medium.
- An all-lowercase task word whose only separator is `-` (`re-index`, `drive-by`, `best-effort`) is a hyphenated task word, not a task identifier, in `ok context`, `ok plan`, `build_context_pack` and `plan_change`, unless the task quotes it in backticks, writes it as a flag (`--allow-network`), or joins it to `/`, `::`, `@`, `=` or a `.` followed by a word (`crates/open-kioku-cor/src/lib.rs`, `drive-by.rs`); a capital, a digit or `_` also makes it an identifier, and a task with an odd number of backticks has no hyphenated task words. A bare package name such as `serde-json` is a hyphenated task word. An unmatched hyphenated word no longer produces the `task identifier(s) name nothing` blocker or its 0.50 cap; it stays in the `anchor` negative evidence, whose reason names it under `hyphenated task word(s) spelled by no selected context`, in a plan's `low confidence:` risk reason as `top context did not spell hyphenated task word(s)`, and in the caveat `N hyphenated task word(s) appear in no selected context`. Labels move both ways: a task whose only unmatched anchors are hyphenated words can read Medium instead of Low (the 0.60 cap for negative evidence still applies), and an unmatched identifier beside a matched hyphenated word, which was counted as a second identifier, now gets the blocker and the 0.50 cap instead of a caveat.
- Context-pack and plan confidence is derived from evidence provenance, not from result prose. `exact_reference_count` and the runtime corroboration count were granted by substring tests over result evidence lines, so a target file defining `scip_setup_report` made `ok context` report `Exact (1.00)` beside `exact_evidence_count: 0`; they now count exact-authority retrieval traces, the impact engine's indexed symbol references, `source_type: scip` evidence, and the typed `runtime_corroboration` component, and `Exact` requires `exact_reference_count > 0`. `negative_evidence_count` is the `primary_context` and `anchor` items of the published `negative_evidence` list on both surfaces instead of a regex over `risk.reasons`; task identifiers that no selected context spells are `anchor` negative evidence, with a 0.50 cap and a blocker naming them when all are missing; a plan's evidence-quality caveats now cap the score like every other caveat, and `exact_reference_available` is reconciled with the typed count. `evidence_density` counts distinct evidence records, and the Markdown line `Exact-evidence selections` is now `Exact-authority selections`. In `ok plan` and `ok context`, tasks naming a uniquely-resolved symbol can read one band higher than before (Medium 0.60 to High 0.94) because the prose count over evidence-quality caveats no longer caps them; `docs/context-pack-spec.md` defines the label semantics.
- Test targets are function, method and test symbols with a test annotation (`#[test]`, `#[tokio::test]`, `#[async_std::test]`, `#[rstest]`, `#[test_case]`, `@Test`, `@ParameterizedTest`, `@RepeatedTest`, `it(`, `test(`, `def test_`) on their first line or in the attribute, annotation and comment stack directly above it, and every symbol in a test-path file, including a repository-root `tests/` or `test/`. Targets are stored at index time.
- `ok verify` and `verify_change` list the innermost symbols whose ranges overlap each diff hunk and report hunks no symbol covers under `changed_regions_without_symbol`; a hunk side with no lines contributes no range. Without a diff, symbols are listed per file with a `symbol_granularity` warning that does not change the verdict. Library callers can supply ranges through `VerifyChangeInput.changed_ranges`.
- `query_evidence_graph` with no `query` and `ok graph schema` return `syntax` (one sentence per clause), `examples` (queries that parse and run) and `unsupported` (rejected forms, each with an example and the accepted alternative), and `edge_types` lists `DerivedFrom`, which queries already accepted. Node and edge types in a query accept the schema's spelling (`DatabaseTable`, `DependsOn`) as well as the serialized one (`database_table`, `DEPENDS_ON`), case-insensitively, where the schema's spelling was rejected before. A parse error names the accepted form: an unknown node or edge type lists the schema's types, a MATCH without an edge pattern shows one, `RETURN f.file_path` says to filter properties in WHERE, a function, DISTINCT or AS alias in RETURN says RETURN accepts only variables bound in MATCH, and trailing input names the token and its column. `ok graph schema --format markdown` escapes Markdown characters in the schema prose so grammar placeholders such as `<path>` render. Every query accepted before is still accepted.
- `ok verify`, `ok contract verify` and `verify_change` check both sides of a rename: the previous path is listed in `changed_files` and held to the forbidden and boundary rules, a copy's source is held to the forbidden rules, the finding names both paths, and a new `previous_paths` field pairs them; `--git` and `--since-plan` diffs are taken with `--find-renames`. Only the new path was checked before. Because the previous path is a changed file, it also feeds changed symbols, recommended tests, changed impact, the API-surface check and the dependency check, matching git's delete-plus-add view of a rename. A rename's hunks are attributed by side (pre-edit lines to the previous path, post-edit lines to the new path), and a side of a text rename with no hunk lists no symbols and no `symbol_granularity` warning. `--check-api-surface` reports a public item whose kind, name and signature are unchanged across a rename as `api_surface_moved`, a warning naming both paths, unless the contract's `api_surface_constraints` forbid removals in the previous path's scope or additions in the new path's scope, in which case the move fails; an item missing from the new path still fails as removed, and a changed signature still fails. Each hunk's content ends where the counts in its `@@ -a,b +c,d @@` header say, so removing a `-- x` line or adding a `++ y` line no longer produces a path or a rename, and a plain `---`/`+++` entry appended after a `diff --git` entry is still a changed file held to the boundary. Rename and copy paths from a CRLF diff carry no trailing `\r`. `verify_change` explanations list the rename and copy pairs. `--git`, `--since-plan`, `ok impact --since` and `ok plan --since` run git with `--no-color --find-renames --src-prefix=a/ --dst-prefix=b/`, so neither `diff.renames`, a local path-prefix setting nor `color.diff` changes what is parsed; copies are detected only in a supplied diff. `ok impact --since` analyses a renamed file's previous path as well as its new one, `ok plan --since` shows a rename as `<new> (from <old>)`, and neither labels a file that follows an added, deleted, renamed or copied file with that file's status.
- `ok setup agent <client> --check` reports `[failed] index` with the storage error, and not ready, when the index exists but cannot be served (unreadable, being built, or written by a newer Open Kioku), instead of exiting with that error before printing any check. (#443)
- `ok path` and MCP `dependency_path` resolve nodes through one function, `open_kioku_graph::resolve_graph_node`, instead of two copies. `ok search` and `search_code` refuse a blank query through one check, `open_kioku_storage::require_search_query`, with one message. (#443)
- Test-target extraction decides whether a file is a test file with the new `open_kioku_core::is_test_code_path`, which applies the `is_test_path` rule ranking already used, so `_test.ts`, `_test.js`, `.test.js`, `.spec.js`, `.test.tsx` and `__tests__/` files yield targets, as `.test.ts`, `.spec.ts`, `test/` and `tests/` files did. A file under `testdata/`, `test_data/`, `test-data/`, `fixtures/`, `__fixtures__/` or `__snapshots__/` holds test inputs, so being there does not make its callables targets. In a test file every function, method and test symbol is a target; variables, constants, classes and modules no longer are. Outside test files the `test` name prefix must end at a word boundary: `test_rounds` and `testRounds` are targets, `testable` is not. In a JavaScript or TypeScript test file, a call that registers a test is also a target, named by its first argument when that is a string or template literal. The recognised calls are `test`, `it`, their modifiers (`test.only`, `it.skip`, `test.each(table)(...)`), and `<identifier>.test` or `<identifier>.it` with a callback argument. The target's range is the call. A template name with a `${...}` substitution has medium confidence, and a call the runner skips (`test.skip`, `it.todo`, `test.failing`, `test.fails`) is low confidence with its own reason and never counts as validation evidence. `describe` and `suite` calls are not targets. Registration targets add no symbol and no graph edge. `TestTarget.origin` records where a target came from: a symbol in a test file, a symbol matched outside one by annotation or name, a registration call, or a disabled registration call. Surfaces that filter validation read that provenance instead of guessing from a name, so a JUnit method (`shouldRoundHalfUp`) and a registered test ("rounds half up") are both plannable. A registration target's id carries its line, so ids change when the calls move and a saved plan's `test:` evidence refs stop resolving after such a re-index.
- A `code_to_test` context pack reports validation evidence as unavailable and returns its primary context when the index holds no test target, or holds none the runner would execute. The pack carries the caveat `no test targets are indexed for this repository` or `every indexed test target is a disabled test the runner skips`, plus `task-family required evidence: validation is unavailable in this repository`, `validation` negative evidence, and a `validation_availability` score of 0.2. It previously returned an empty pack with `missing_required_evidence:validation`. Availability is decided from the indexed targets alone, so the diagnostics never call validation unavailable while the pack carries a validation target. A validation stream that runs over indexed targets and matches none of them still blocks. On a repository with no usable target every pack lists `validation` as attempted but not succeeded.
- `ok plan` keeps tests whose names its heuristics used to drop: a test registered by a JavaScript or TypeScript runner call, and a JUnit or `unittest` method named for behaviour rather than containing "test". Such repositories planned no validation and fell back to the manual-validation placeholder while the context pack listed the same targets, and `ok verify` then reported each recommended test as missing from the plan. `ok plan` and `ok verify` now call one function, `open_kioku_plan::select_validation_targets`, which applies the same filter, the same per-file retention of class-like names, and the same cap, so the two surfaces cannot disagree about a repository's validation.
- A test the runner skips is excluded wherever validation evidence is counted: `find_tests_for_change` and `ok tests` no longer return one, the selector never raises one to the Required tier however strong the overlap evidence, and `ok status` counts only targets that count as evidence, so a repository of `test.todo` stubs no longer reads as ready while every pack reports validation unavailable.
- `ok trust` distinguishes an unreadable tests table from a repository without tests: the storage error becomes a caveat naming it and the missing-test list is omitted, instead of every source file being reported as untested.
- `query_evidence_graph` and `ok graph query` read each WHERE field from the node or edge it names. `source`, `source_type` and `confidence` parsed on node variables and matched no row, because graph nodes carry no evidence; a one-hop edge can now be bound in MATCH and filtered on its evidence as `evidence_source`, `evidence_source_type` and `confidence` (`MATCH (a:Function)-[c:CALLS]->(b:Function) WHERE c.confidence >= 0.85 RETURN a, b`), `confidence` also takes `<`, `<=`, `>` and `>=` and compares the band's score (low 0.35, medium 0.6, high 0.85, exact 1.0) as a number, and those fields on a node variable are a `parse_error` naming the edge binding. `file_path` and `qualified_name` compared every node's label: `file_path` is now a File node's label and, on a symbol node, the path of the File node that defines it, and `qualified_name` is a symbol node's label. A field the variable's type does not take (`qualified_name` on a File node, `file_path` on a `ConfigKey`), an unknown `confidence` band and an unknown `evidence_source_type` are `parse_error`s listing what is accepted; when a matched node carries no `file_path` or `qualified_name` (a `Module` built from an import, a symbol no single File node defines), its row is excluded and a caveat reports at least how many scanned rows that cost, since the scan stops once the requested page is filled. An `evidence_source` value naming a node rather than a recording pass is rejected as well, instead of returning an empty result that reads as "nothing links these". A `file_path` filter reads the defining files of each scanned batch of candidate rows in one `GraphStore::edges_by_type_for_nodes` read (a new trait method, implemented by the SQLite store; a store whose default returns `Unsupported` is read once per symbol). In the schema, `syntax` lists the filter fields per node type, `examples` adds a symbol `file_path` query and an edge-evidence query, `unsupported` replaces `edge_variable` with `edge_variable_in_return` and adds `edge_variable_on_hop_range`, `field_the_node_type_does_not_carry` and `evidence_field_on_a_node`, and `query_features` adds `edge_evidence_filters` and `numeric_confidence_comparison`. A query that filtered a symbol's `file_path` or a File node's `qualified_name` against the label now returns different rows or a parse error.

## [4.0.0] — 2026-09-11

Two things need doing on upgrade, both detailed first below: **run `ok index` to rebuild every existing index**, and **reconfigure any agent that names an MCP tool by one of the retired names** (`ok setup agent --apply` rewrites its own guidance for you).

### Breaking

- Four public enums gain a variant, so a downstream `match` over any of them without a wildcard arm stops compiling: `GraphEdgeType::DerivedFrom`, `RelationshipProofKind::DeclaredOrigin` and `RetrievalSourceKind::DerivedSibling` (#381), and `SkipSource::Parser` (#350). None is `#[non_exhaustive]`, so this is a semver break rather than an additive change; serialized data is unaffected in both directions, since the new names only ever appear in newly written indexes and older readers never emitted them.
- **Every existing index must be rebuilt: run `ok index` after upgrading.** The index storage format changed: SQLite `user_version` 3 -> 4 and `IndexManifest.schema_version` 1 -> 2. `graph_edges` and `call_sites` no longer carry a JSON document per row beside the query columns holding the same values; every field now has a typed column and every repeated string is written once into a per-table dictionary (`graph_strings`, `call_site_strings`) and referenced by integer id. Opening a pre-4.0 index detects the old layout by the `json` column (a `PRAGMA table_info` check, not a scan), drops those two tables, and makes every relationship read report ``run `ok index` to rebuild them`` rather than answering from an empty table — an empty answer would read as "no such relationship exists". The detection cannot fire twice, because the column it keys on is gone afterwards. `ok snapshot export` refuses a store awaiting the rebuild, `ok snapshot import` refuses an artifact exported from a pre-4.0 index, and the cross-project workspace linker refuses a member index awaiting it — each names the fix instead of producing something that only looks complete. Search, symbol lookup and the file inventory keep working on the old index; it is relationship evidence specifically that is withheld until `ok index` runs, and the surfaces built on it — `ok impact`, `ok plan`, `ok preflight`, `ok context` and MCP `impact_analysis`, `plan_change`, `build_context_pack` — refuse with that instruction rather than answer from the empty graph. The same index fails `ok doctor`'s `graph` check, is named by `ok setup audit`, and reports `graph_rebuild_required: true` in `ok --json status` and MCP `repo_status`; the analysis-semantics fingerprint those surfaces also report is unchanged since 3.1.0 and does not see it. (#363)
- The advertised MCP tool surface is 16 tools, down from 58 (#406). Every advertised tool now answers one question no other tool answers. Removal is outright: retired names left `tools/list` and the dispatch table in the same change, so an agent holding a stale name from a cached config or an old prompt gets an error rather than a response whose shape no longer matches the description that name was chosen from. A `RETIRED_TOOLS` table asserts that none of them can reappear on either surface, and carries the replacement each one points at (below).
  - **Folded into the sixteen, no capability lost (23 names).** `repo_status` absorbs `list_languages` (as a `languages` field, taken from index coverage where it exists) and `semantic_status` (as `semantic_lifecycle`, which now carries the whole `SemanticStatus` — provider, backend, model, model artifact hash, dimensions and counts included — rather than the readiness summary it held before, so an agent can still ask which model produced the vectors it is about to trust; `ok semantic status` is the CLI equivalent). `list_files` absorbs `explain_file` as a per-path detail mode. `search_code` absorbs `search_files`, `semantic_search`, `hybrid_search` and `explain_search_result` behind `mode` (`code`, `graph`, `semantic`, `hybrid`); no `explain` parameter was added, because `score_breakdown` and `evidence_refs` already ship on every result and a flag that toggled nothing would be a new false claim. `search_symbols` absorbs `list_symbols` by making `query` optional. `get_definition` absorbs `get_symbol_context` behind `include_body` and `explain_symbol`, which was already the same call. `get_references` absorbs `get_callers`, `get_callees` and `get_implementations` behind `kind`. `dependency_path` absorbs `module_dependencies` by making `to` optional. `build_context_pack` absorbs `build_compressed_context` behind `compress`. `plan_change` absorbs `preflight_change` and `propose_patch` behind `detail`, and `create_change_contract` behind `persist` (with `store` for a transient contract, and `plan`/`plan_json` so a saved plan becomes a contract without re-planning). `verify_change` absorbs `verify_change_contract` (`contract_id`, `contract`, `contract_json`) and `explain_verification` (`verification`, `verification_json`, or `explain: true` on a contract verification). `find_tests_for_change` absorbs `recommend_validation_plan` and `explain_test_coverage` by making `path` optional. `query_evidence_graph` absorbs `get_evidence_schema`: called with no `query` it returns the schema.
  - **`get_references` response shape.** The merged tool returns an object, not a bare occurrence array. Each evidence kind is its own section naming its own `evidence_source` — `symbol_occurrences`, `sqlite_graph_store`, `persisted_implements_facts` — with its own caveats and its own payload key (`occurrences`, `nodes`/`edges`, `implementations`).
